contains a monomial with some odd exponent, while for $\Gamma_{\rm rand}$
and $\Gamma_{\rm bip}$ the centre of $A_q(\Gamma)$ is the polynomial ring
$k[x_v^2:v\in\Gamma]$.
{\rm(iii)} If the adjacency matrix of $\Gamma$ has infinite
rank over $\Fq_2$, in particular for $\Gamma_{\rm rand}$ and
$\Gamma_{\rm bip}$, then $D_q(\Gamma)$ satisfies no polynomial identity and
is infinite-dimensional over its centre; if moreover $k$ has positive
characteristic, then the theory of the division ring $D_q(\Gamma)$ in the
ring language is not simple.
{\rm(iv)} The reduct of any model of $T_q(\Gamma)$ to the vector sort with
each $x_v$ ($v\in\Gamma$) as a unary function symbol, without the vertex
sort, is a module over $A_q(\Gamma)$; its theory is stable and every
formula in it is equivalent to a Boolean combination of positive primitive
formulas.  Consequently every formula of $T_q(\Gamma)$ in which the vertex
variables are replaced by named constants and no vertex quantifier occurs
is stable.  Moreover the module reducts of $M^{(n)}_q(\Gamma)$ for
$1\le n\le\omega$ are all elementarily equivalent, so infinite
dimensionality over $D_q(\Gamma)$ is not expressible in the module reduct.
{\rm(v)} Let $\psi(w;u)$ be the formula $\exists v\,(w=x_vu)$ and
$\theta(w;u)$ the formula $\exists v\exists v'\,(w=x_vx_{v'}u)$, both with
vector variables $w,u$ and no parameters.  Then $\psi$ has no order pattern
of length three in any model of $T_q(\Gamma)$, hence is stable, for every
$\Gamma$.  For distinct vertices $a,c$ and any nonzero vector $b$,
$\theta(x_ab,\,-x_c^{-1}b)$ holds if and only if $aEc$.  Consequently, if
$\Gamma$ contains for every $n$ distinct vertices $a_1,\dots,a_n,c_1,\dots,c_n$
with $a_iEc_j$ if and only if $i\le j$, as $\Gamma_{\rm bip}$ and
$\Gamma_{\rm rand}$ do, then $\theta$ has the order property in
$T_q(\Gamma)$; and if $E$ has the independence property on distinct
vertices of $\Gamma$, then so does $\theta$ in $T_q(\Gamma)$.
{\rm(vi)} A supersimple theory without stable forking is not one-based.  In
particular the theory of the counterexample of Freitag and Mutchnik is not
one-based.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
{\rm(i)} For finite $\Gamma=\{v_1<\dots<v_n\}$, $A_q(\Gamma)$ is the iterated
Ore extension $k[x_{v_1}][x_{v_2};\sigma_2]\cdots[x_{v_n};\sigma_n]$ where
$\sigma_j$ multiplies $x_{v_i}$ by $q$ or $1$ according to adjacency; the Poincar\'e--Birkhoff--Witt basis, the domain property and the Noetherian
property follow, and a Noetherian domain is
Ore; the defining relations are symmetric in each pair, so no orientation
of the edges is needed, and they are homogeneous, so the algebra is graded
by the exponent vector.  For countable $\Gamma$ the algebra is the directed union of the
algebras of its finite induced subgraphs, each embedded in the next by the
basis description, and a directed union of Ore domains is an Ore domain
because the Ore condition concerns two elements at a time.  The functoriality
and the automorphism extension follow from the universal property of Ore
localisation, and the last claim from the universality of the
random graph.
{\rm(ii)} Multiplying a monomial $x^a$ by $x_v$ on the left and on the right
gives $x_vx^a=(-1)^{\sum_{uEv}a_u}x^ax_v$, so $x_v$ commutes with a linear
combination of distinct monomials if and only if it commutes with each, and
$x^a$ is central if and only if every such exponent sum is even.  An
alternating form on an odd-dimensional space over $\Fq_2$ is singular, which
gives the odd-order clause.  For the random graph, given $a$ with some odd
entry $a_u$, let $S$ be the set of vertices with odd exponent; the extension
property gives a vertex $v$ adjacent to $u$ and to no other element of $S$,
so $\sum_{u'Ev}a_{u'}$ is odd; for the random bipartite graph take $v$ on the
side opposite to $u$, adjacent to $u$ and to no other vertex of $S$ on the
side of $u$, and note that $v$ is adjacent to no vertex on its own side.
Thus the central monomials are exactly those with all exponents even.
{\rm(iii)} If
the adjacency form has rank $2r$ on a finite induced subgraph $H$, a
symplectic basis of the form on $\Fq_2^H$ gives $2r$ monomials
$y_1,z_1,\dots,y_r,z_r$ in the generators of $H$, with $y_iz_i=-z_iy_i$ and
all other pairs commuting, whose squares are central; the subalgebra they generate is a quantum
affine space with these relations, a domain whose division ring of
fractions has centre $k(y_i^2,z_i^2:i\le r)$ and, by the basis of monomials
with exponents $0$ or $1$, dimension $4^r$ over it, the centre of the division ring of fractions
being the field of fractions of the centre by Posner's theorem, so it is
a tensor product of $r$ quaternion algebras over its centre, of polynomial identity
degree $2^r$.  A ring satisfying a polynomial
identity of degree $d$ passes it to every subring, and a division ring
finite-dimensional over its centre satisfies one, so unbounded $r$ excludes
both.  In positive characteristic, a division
ring whose theory is NTP$_2$, in particular one whose theory is simple, is
finite-dimensional over its centre by a theorem of Milliet,
which gives the last claim.
{\rm(iv)} The reduct is a module over the ring $A_q(\Gamma)$ in the usual
language of modules; the Baur--Monk theorem gives elimination of quantifiers
down to positive primitive formulas and stability.  A
formula of $T_q(\Gamma)$ with vertex parameters and without vertex
quantifiers is a formula of this reduct.  For the elementary equivalence,
two modules are elementarily equivalent when the indices $|\varphi(M)/\psi(M)|$ agree
for all pairs of positive primitive formulas $\psi\to\varphi$ in one free variable,
each index counted as $1$, a finite number or $\infty$.  A
positive primitive subgroup is invariant under every endomorphism of the
module, and for $M=D_q(\Gamma)^{(n)}$ the right multiplications by
column-finite matrices over $D_q(\Gamma)$ are endomorphisms which carry any
nonzero vector to any vector; hence the positive primitive subgroups of $M$
in one variable are $0$ and $M$.  Positive primitive formulas commute with
direct sums, so $\varphi(D_q(\Gamma)^{(n)})=0$ if and only if
$\varphi(D_q(\Gamma))=0$, and all indices agree across $n$, each being $1$
or $\infty$.
{\rm(v)} We use that scalars act faithfully on nonzero vectors: $db=d'b$
with $b\ne0$ forces $d=d'$, and $x_v$ is invertible in $D_q(\Gamma)$.  First a
lemma: if $x_sx_t^{-1}=x_{s'}x_{t'}^{-1}$ in $D_q(\Gamma)$ for vertices
$s,t,s',t'$, then $(s,t)=(s',t')$ or $s=t$ and $s'=t'$.  Indeed
$x_sx_t^{-1}x_{t'}=x_{s'}$, and $x_t^{-1}x_{t'}=\eps x_{t'}x_t^{-1}$ with
$\eps=\pm1$, so $\eps x_sx_{t'}=x_{s'}x_t$ in $A_q(\Gamma)$; both sides are
homogeneous of degree one in each of their generators, so
$\{s,t'\}=\{s',t\}$ as multisets, which is the claim.  Now suppose
$w_1,w_2,w_3,u_1,u_2,u_3$ satisfy $\psi(w_i;u_j)$ exactly when $i\le j$.
From $\psi(w_1;u_j)$ there are vertices $v_j$ with $w_1=x_{v_j}u_j$.  If
$w_1=0$ then every $u_j$ is $0$ and $\psi(w_3;u_2)$ and $\psi(w_3;u_3)$
have the same truth value, a contradiction; so $u_j=x_{v_j}^{-1}w_1$ with
$w_1\ne0$.  From $\psi(w_2;u_2)$ and $\psi(w_2;u_3)$ there are $v',v''$ with
$x_{v'}x_{v_2}^{-1}=x_{v''}x_{v_3}^{-1}$.  By the lemma either
$v_2=v_3$, so $u_2=u_3$ and again $\psi(w_3;u_2)$, $\psi(w_3;u_3)$ agree, or
$v'=v_2$, so $w_2=w_1$ and $\psi(w_2;u_1)$ holds, a contradiction in both
cases.  A formula with no order pattern of length three is stable.  The facts used, namely that each $x_v$ acts bijectively on
$V$, that scalars act faithfully on nonzero vectors, the lemma and the
sign identity below, are expressed by sentences with vertex quantifiers
that hold in $M_q(\Gamma)$, so they hold in every model.  For
$\theta$: $\theta(x_ab,-x_c^{-1}b)$ says that $x_a=-x_vx_{v'}x_c^{-1}$ for
some $v,v'$, that is, $x_ax_c=-x_vx_{v'}$ in $A_q(\Gamma)$; by the grading
$\{v,v'\}=\{a,c\}$, and $(v,v')=(a,c)$ would give $2x_ax_c=0$, impossible
in a domain of characteristic different from two, so $(v,v')=(c,a)$ and
the condition is $x_ax_c=-x_cx_a$, which holds exactly when $aEc$.  Given
the vertices $a_i,c_j$, the vectors $w_i=x_{a_i}b$ and $u_j=-x_{c_j}^{-1}b$
satisfy $\theta(w_i;u_j)$ exactly when $i\le j$; the independence property
transfers in the same way.  In $\Gamma_{\rm bip}$ and $\Gamma_{\rm rand}$
the required configurations exist by the extension axioms, with the $a_i$
and the $c_j$ on opposite sides in the bipartite case.
{\rm(vi)} Supersimple theories eliminate hyperimaginaries,
and a one-based simple theory that eliminates hyperimaginaries has stable
forking.  Freitag and Mutchnik prove that their theory is
supersimple and lacks stable forking.
\end{proof}

\begin{conjecture}[Finite-rank stable forking]\label{au1}
Every supersimple theory of finite $SU$-rank has stable forking.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Rank-one stable forking]\label{au2}
Let $T$ be simple, $A\subseteq B$, and $p\in S(B)$ a type of $SU$-rank one
that forks over $A$.  Then some stable formula in $p$ forks over $A$.  Granted
Conjecture~\ref{au9}(i), the rank bound is sharp: the types there have
$SU$-rank two and fork over their base without any stable forking
formula.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Stable forking for countably categorical simple theories]\label{au3}
Every $\aleph_0$-categorical simple theory has stable forking.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[The counterexample is two-dependent]\label{au4}
{\rm(i)} For every countable graph $\Gamma$ and every $k\ge1$,
$T_q(\Gamma)$ is $k$-dependent if and only if $\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma)$ is
$k$-dependent.
{\rm(ii)} In particular $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$ and $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm rand})$
are $2$-dependent and not NIP, so the stable forking conjecture fails in a
$2$-dependent supersimple theory.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Transfer of dividing lines from graphs to quantum modules]\label{au5}
For every countable graph $\Gamma$ the following hold.
{\rm(i)} $T_q(\Gamma)$ is simple if and only if $\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma)$ is
simple, and supersimple if and only if $\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma)$ is supersimple.
{\rm(ii)} $T_q(\Gamma)$ is NSOP$_1$ if and only if $\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma)$ is
NSOP$_1$.
{\rm(iii)} $T_q(\Gamma)$ is stable if and only if $\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma)$ is
stable.
{\rm(iv)} If $\Gamma$ is infinite and $\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma)$ is supersimple
of $SU$-rank one, then $T_q(\Gamma)$ is supersimple of $SU$-rank $\omega$,
the type of a vector outside the span of the parameters having rank
exactly $\omega$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Stable forking dividing line for quantum modules]\label{au6}
Let $\Gamma$ be a countable graph with simple theory.  Then $T_q(\Gamma)$
has stable forking if and only if $\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma)$ is stable.
Moreover, when $\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma)$ is unstable the failure is witnessed by
the type of a single vector over a set consisting of finitely many vectors
and finitely many vertices.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Global linear disjointness is forking in every quantum module]\label{au7}
Let $\Gamma$ be a countable graph with simple theory, and work in a
monster model of $T_q(\Gamma)$ with vertex sort $\Gamma^*$.  For a set $A$
write $A_\Gamma$ for its vertex part, $A_V$ for its vector part,
$\langle A\rangle$ for the $D_q(\Gamma^*)$-span of $A_V$, and
$\operatorname{cl}(A)$ for the pair consisting of the vertex part of
$\operatorname{acl}(A)$ and the $D_q(\operatorname{acl}(A)_\Gamma)$-span of
$A_V$.  Then for all $A,B,C$,
\[
 A\forkind_C B
 \iff
 \langle AC\rangle\cap\langle BC\rangle=\langle C\rangle
 \ \text{ and }\ 
 \operatorname{cl}(AC)_\Gamma\forkind_{\operatorname{cl}(C)_\Gamma}\operatorname{cl}(BC)_\Gamma
 \ \text{ in }\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma),
\]
where the last independence is forking independence in $\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma)$;
and $T_q(\Gamma)$ has quantifier elimination up to closure, in the sense
that for tuples $\bar a,\bar a'$ and a set $C=\operatorname{cl}(C)$,
$\operatorname{tp}(\bar a/C)=\operatorname{tp}(\bar a'/C)$ if and only if
there is an isomorphism $\operatorname{cl}(\bar aC)\to\operatorname{cl}(\bar a'C)$
of two-sorted structures over $C$ carrying $\bar a$ to $\bar a'$ whose
vertex part is an elementary map of $\Gamma^*$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Where one-basedness fails in the counterexample]\label{au8}
In $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$:
{\rm(i)} for every tuple of vectors $\bar w$ and every boundedly closed
set $C$ there is a finite tuple $\bar y$ of vertices of
$\operatorname{acl}(\bar wC)$ such that the canonical base of
$\operatorname{tp}(\bar w/C)$ is contained in $\operatorname{bdd}(\bar w\bar y)$;
{\rm(ii)} the theory is CM-trivial, so that it is $1$-ample but not
$2$-ample.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Failure of stable forking over models]\label{au9}
{\rm(i)} In $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$ let $M$ be a model, $b$ a vector
outside $\langle M\rangle$, $m\in M$ a vector, and $y,y'$ distinct vertices
not in $\operatorname{acl}(Mb)$.  Then the type of $w=x_yx_{y'}b+m$ over
$Mb$ forks over $M$ and contains no stable formula that forks over $M$; in
particular $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$ does not have stable forking over
models.
{\rm(ii)} For every countable graph $\Gamma$ whose theory is NSOP$_1$ and
unstable, $T_q(\Gamma)$ fails the weak stable Kim-forking property: some
type over a set $B$ containing a model $M$ Kim-forks over $M$ while no
stable formula over $B$ in the type Kim-forks over $M$.  In particular the
proposal that every NSOP$_1$ theory has the weak stable Kim-forking
property fails already for simple theories.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Decidability and effective forking]\label{au10}
For $k=\Q$, the theories $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$ and $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm rand})$
are decidable; given a formula $\varphi(x,\bar y,\bar z)$ and tuples
$\bar a,\bar b$ from the computable presentation of the prime model, it is
decidable whether $\varphi(x,\bar a,\bar b)$ forks over $\bar a$; and both
theories admit a computable normal form for types over finite sets, namely
the isomorphism type of the closure of the tuple over the closure of the
parameters in the sense of Conjecture~\ref{au7}.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[The theories of the scalar division rings are not simple]\label{au11}
For every field $k$ of characteristic different from two and every
countable graph $\Gamma$ whose adjacency form has infinite rank over $\Fq_2$,
the theory of the division ring $D_q(\Gamma)$ in the ring language is not
simple.  In particular the theory of the scalars of the counterexample
is not simple although the theory of the module is.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Stable formulas and hidden vertices]\label{au12}
Let $\Gamma$ be a countable graph with simple theory.
Vertex parameters are treated throughout this statement as named
constants, formulas are in vector variables, and stability is asserted
for every partition of the vector variables into an object block and a
parameter block.
{\rm(i)} Every formula of $T_q(\Gamma)$ with at most one vertex quantifier
is stable.
{\rm(ii)} For $n\ge2$ and integers $a_1,\dots,a_n\ge1$ of which at least
two are odd, the formula
$\exists v_1\cdots\exists v_n\,(v_i\ne v_j\ (i<j)\wedge w=x_{v_1}^{a_1}\cdots x_{v_n}^{a_n}u)$
has the order property in $T_q(\Gamma)$ whenever $\Gamma$ contains the
half-graph configurations of Proposition~\ref{au:basic}(v).
{\rm(iii)} If $\mathrm{Th}(\Gamma)$ is unstable, then a formula is stable
if and only if it is equivalent
to a Boolean combination of formulas of the form
$\exists v\,(\chi(v,\bar c)\wedge\rho(\bar w,\bar u,x_v,\bar x_{\bar c}))$,
where $\chi$ is a formula of the graph with vertex parameters $\bar c$
and $\rho$ is a positive primitive formula of the module reduct in which
$x_v$ and the generators at $\bar c$ are the only scalars.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Nonforking extensions do not restore stable witnesses]\label{au13}
{\rm(i)} Let $T$ be simple.  If for every $A\subseteq B$ and every
$p\in S(B)$ forking over $A$ there are $C\supseteq B$ and a nonforking
extension $r\in S(C)$ of $p$ containing a formula $\varphi(x,c)$ with
$\varphi(x;y)$ stable that forks over $A$, then $T$ has stable forking;
the converse holds trivially.
{\rm(ii)} In $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$, with $M$, $b$, $m$, $y$, $y'$ and
$w=x_yx_{y'}b+m$ as in Conjecture~\ref{au9}(i), no nonforking extension of
the type of $w$ over $Mb$ contains a stable formula that forks over $M$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Definable groups in quantum modules]\label{au14}
Every group interpretable in $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$ or in
$T_q(\Gamma_{\rm rand})$ is abelian-by-finite, and every infinite such group
has a definable subgroup of finite index that is definably isomorphic to
a quotient of a definable subgroup of $V^n$ by a definable subgroup, for
some $n$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Countable models of the counterexample]\label{au15}
For $k=\Q$, the structures $M^{(n)}_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$ for
$1\le n\le\omega$ are pairwise elementarily equivalent and pairwise
non-isomorphic, and every countable model of $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$ is
isomorphic to one of them.  In particular $T_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$ has
exactly $\aleph_0$ countable models, the prime model is
$M^{(1)}_q(\Gamma_{\rm bip})$, and the theory is not $\aleph_0$-categorical
although its vertex sort is.
\end{conjecture}

\part{Percolation at the critical point in every dimension}

\medskip\noindent\emph{Added to the deposit: 5 September 2026.}\par\medskip

This part states twenty conjectures on critical percolation on $\Z^d$ once the critical point is known to carry no infinite cluster.  The proved layer is Proposition~\ref{av:basic}.

Throughout, $d\ge2$, $\Pp_p$ is nearest-neighbour bond percolation on
$\Z^d$ with parameter $p$, $p_c=p_c(\Z^d)$, $\Lambda_n=[-n,n]^d\cap\Z^d$
with boundary $\partial\Lambda_n=\Lambda_n\setminus\Lambda_{n-1}$, and
$B_n$ is the ball of radius $n$ in the graph distance, with
$\partial B_n=B_n\setminus B_{n-1}$; we put $\pi_d(0)=1$.  We write
$C$ for the open cluster of the origin,
$\theta(p)=\Pp_p(|C|=\infty)$, $\theta_n(p)=\Pp_p(0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n)$,
$\pi_d(n)=\theta_n(p_c)$ for the critical one-arm probability,
$\tau_p(0,x)=\Pp_p(0\leftrightarrow x)$ for the two-point function,
$\chi(p)=\E_p|C|$ and $\kappa(p)=\E_p(1/|C|)$ for the mean cluster size
and the number of clusters per site.  The critical exponents are
defined, when the limits exist, by
$\theta(p)=(p-p_c)^{\beta_d+o(1)}$ as $p\downarrow p_c$,
$\chi(p)=(p_c-p)^{-\gamma_d+o(1)}$ and $\xi(p)=(p_c-p)^{-\nu_d+o(1)}$
as $p\uparrow p_c$, where $\xi(p)$ is the correlation length given by
the exponential decay rate of $\tau_p(0,x)$, $\pi_d(n)=n^{-\rho_d+o(1)}$,
$\tau_{p_c}(0,x)=|x|^{-(d-2+\eta_d)+o(1)}$, and
$\Pp_{p_c}(|C|\ge n)=n^{-1/\delta_d+o(1)}$; when both exist,
$\rho_d=\beta_d/\nu_d$.  The values established for site percolation on
the triangular lattice, $\beta=5/36$, $\nu=4/3$, $\rho=5/48$,
$\eta=5/24$, are expected
for every planar lattice but are not proved for $\Z^2$; the numerical
values used below, $\beta_3\approx0.42$, $\nu_3\approx0.88$,
$\eta_3\approx-0.05$, $\beta_4\approx0.66$, $\nu_4\approx0.69$,
$\beta_5\approx0.83$, $\nu_5\approx0.57$, and the mean-field values
$\beta=1$, $\nu=1/2$, $\eta=0$, $\rho=2$, $\delta=2$ for $d\ge7$, with
$d=6$ the upper critical dimension, are taken
from; the mean-field
values are theorems for $d\ge11$.
Where a planar exponent is called a theorem below, the theorem is for
site percolation on the triangular lattice.  The scaling function of the
one-arm event, when it exists, is the function $\Phi_d$ with
$\theta_n(p_c+tn^{-1/\nu_d})=\pi_d(n)\Phi_d(t)(1+o(1))$ for fixed $t$.
Dynamical percolation refreshes every edge independently at rate one,
keeping the marginal $\Pp_{p_c}$; an exceptional time is a time at which
an infinite open cluster exists.

\begin{proposition}[Uniform finite-volume approximation and the critical one-arm bound]\label{av:basic}
{\rm(i)} For every $d\ge2$ the following are equivalent: $\theta(p_c)=0$;
$\theta$ is continuous on $[0,1]$; $\theta_n\to\theta$ uniformly on
$[0,1]$.  In particular, given the vanishing theorem,
$\sup_{p\in[0,1]}(\theta_n(p)-\theta(p))\to0$.
{\rm(ii)} For $\eps\in(0,1)$ let $p_n(\eps)$ be the unique $p$ with
$\theta_n(p)=\eps$ and $p_\eps=\inf\{p:\theta(p)\ge\eps\}$.  Then
$p_n(\eps)$ is nondecreasing in $n$ and $p_n(\eps)\uparrow p_\eps$;
given the vanishing theorem, $p_n(\eps)>p_c$ for all large $n$.
{\rm(iii)} For all $x\ne0$, $\tau_{p_c}(0,x)\le\pi_d(\lfloor|x|_\infty/2\rfloor)^2$,
and for all $n\ge1$,
\[
 \sum_{x\in\partial\Lambda_n}\tau_{p_c}(0,x)\ge\frac1{2dp_c},\qquad
 \pi_d(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)\ge\bigl(2dp_c|\partial\Lambda_n|\bigr)^{-1/2}\ge c_dn^{-(d-1)/2}.
\]
Consequently, whenever $\rho_d$ exists, $\rho_d\le(d-1)/2$.
{\rm(iv)} $\lim_{d\to\infty}\Pp_{p_c(\Z^d)}(0\leftrightarrow\partial B_1)=1-\e^{-1}$,
the survival probability to the first generation of the critical
Galton--Watson process with Poisson offspring of mean one.
{\rm(v)} In the plane, for $n=1$ and every $p\ne1/2$,
$\Pp_p(0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_1,\ |C|<\infty)<\Pp_{1/2}(0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_1)$.
{\rm(vi)} Let $G_j$ be transitive graphs of degree $D_j\to\infty$ and girth
tending to infinity with $D_jp_c(G_j)\to1$.  Then for every fixed $n$,
$\Pp_{p_c(G_j)}(0\leftrightarrow\partial B_n)\to s_n$, the survival
probability to generation $n$ of the critical Galton--Watson process with
Poisson offspring of mean one.
{\rm(vii)} For every fixed $n\ge1$,
$\Pp_{p_c(\Z^d)}(0\leftrightarrow\partial B_n)=s_n+O_n(1/d)$ as $d\to\infty$,
with $s_n$ as in {\rm(vi)}; in particular the limit is $s_n$, and
$s_n\sim2/n$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
{\rm(i)} By van den Berg and Keane (see also
Grimmett), $\theta$ is right-continuous on
$[0,1]$ and left-continuous on $(p_c,1]$, and it vanishes on $[0,p_c)$;
hence $\theta$ is continuous on $[0,1]$ if and only if it is
left-continuous at $p_c$, that is, if and only if $\theta(p_c)=0$.  Each
$\theta_n$ is a polynomial in $p$, the sequence $\theta_n$ is
nonincreasing in $n$, and $\theta_n\to\theta$ pointwise since
$\{0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n\}$ decreases to $\{|C|=\infty\}$.  If
$\theta$ is continuous, Dini's theorem on the compact interval $[0,1]$
gives uniform convergence; conversely a uniform limit of continuous
functions is continuous.
{\rm(ii)} $\theta_n$ is a polynomial, strictly increasing on $(0,1)$ since
$\{0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n\}$ is increasing with probability
strictly between $0$ and $1$, so $p_n(\eps)$ is well defined.  Since
$\theta_{n+1}\le\theta_n$, $p_{n+1}(\eps)\ge p_n(\eps)$, and since
$\theta_n\ge\theta$ and $\theta(p_\eps)\ge\eps$ by right-continuity,
$p_n(\eps)\le p_\eps$.  For $p<p_\eps$ one has $\theta(p)<\eps$, so
$\theta_n(p)<\eps$ for large $n$ and $p_n(\eps)>p$; hence
$p_n(\eps)\uparrow p_\eps$.  If $\theta(p_c)=0$ then $\theta_n(p_c)\to0$,
so $\theta_n(p_c)<\eps$ and $p_n(\eps)>p_c$ for large $n$.
{\rm(iii)} Let $m=\lfloor|x|_\infty/2\rfloor$.  An open path from $0$ to
$x$ leaves $\Lambda_m$ at a first point and continues to $x$, and
every point of $\partial\Lambda_m$ is at $\ell^\infty$-distance at least
$|x|_\infty-m\ge m$ from $x$, so
$\{0\leftrightarrow x\}\subseteq\{0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_m\}\circ\{x\leftrightarrow x+\partial\Lambda_m\}$
(the two path segments are edge-disjoint: every edge of the first has an
endpoint in $\Lambda_{m-1}$, while $|x|_\infty\ge2m$ gives
$(x+\Lambda_m)\cap\Lambda_{m-1}=\emptyset$, so no edge of the second does;
for $|x|_\infty=1$ the bound is trivial with $\pi_d(0)=1$),
and the van den Berg--Kesten inequality with
translation invariance gives the first bound.  For the second, Duminil-Copin
and Tassion proved that if
$\varphi_p(S)=p\sum_{x\in S,\ y\notin S,\ x\sim y}\Pp_p(0\leftrightarrow x\text{ in }S)<1$
for some finite $S\ni0$ then $\theta_n(p)$ decays exponentially and
$p<p_c$; since $\varphi_p(S)$ is continuous in $p$ and $\theta(p)>0$ for
$p>p_c$, $\varphi_{p_c}(S)\ge1$ for every finite $S\ni0$.  With
$S=\Lambda_n$, every boundary vertex has at most $2d$ neighbours outside
$S$ and $\Pp(0\leftrightarrow x\text{ in }S)\le\tau_{p_c}(0,x)$, which
gives the sum bound.  Each term is at most
$\pi_d(\lfloor n/2\rfloor)^2$ by the first bound, and
$|\partial\Lambda_n|\le2d(2n+1)^{d-1}$, which gives the rest.
{\rm(iv)} $\Pp_{p_c}(0\leftrightarrow\partial B_1)=1-(1-p_c)^{2d}$, and
$2dp_c(\Z^d)\to1$ by Kesten's asymptotics, so the limit is
$1-\e^{-1}$, which is the probability that a Poisson variable of mean one
is positive.
{\rm(v)} Write $f(p)=\Pp_p(0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_1,|C|<\infty)=1-(1-p)^4-\theta(p)$.
For $p<1/2$, $f(p)=1-(1-p)^4<1-2^{-4}=f(1/2)$.  For $p>1/2$, with
$u=p-1/2$, the mean-field lower bound in the explicit form of
Duminil-Copin and Tassion,
$\theta(p)\ge(p-p_c)/(p(1-p_c))$, with $p_c=1/2$ by Kesten's
theorem and $\theta(1/2)=0$ by Harris,
gives $\theta(p)\ge2u/p\ge2u$, while
$(1-p)^4\ge(1/2)^4-4u(1/2)^3=2^{-4}-u/2$; hence
$f(p)\le1-2^{-4}+u/2-2u<f(1/2)$.
{\rm(vi)} If the girth exceeds $2n+1$, the ball $B_n$ of $G_j$ is a tree in
which every vertex other than the root has $D_j-1$ children, and the event
$\{0\leftrightarrow\partial B_n\}$ depends only on the edges of $B_n$; its
probability is the survival probability to generation $n$ of the
branching process with offspring law $\mathrm{Bin}(D_j,p)$ at the root and
$\mathrm{Bin}(D_j-1,p)$ afterwards, a fixed finite composition of the
generating functions.  At $p=p_c(G_j)$ these laws converge to Poisson of
mean one as $D_jp_c(G_j)\to1$, and the finite composition converges to
$s_n$; for fixed degree and girth tending to infinity the corresponding
statement, with the critical point tending to $1/(D-1)$, is the theorem
of Benjamini, Nachmias and Peres.
{\rm(vii)} Explore the cluster of the origin in $\Z^d$ generation by
generation: the root examines its $2d$ edges, every later vertex the
$2d-1$ edges other than the one through which it was reached, and a
vertex reached through an open edge is added to the set $S$ of seen
vertices unless it is already there.  Couple this with the branching
process of {\rm(vi)} (offspring $\mathrm{Bin}(2d,p)$ at the root and
$\mathrm{Bin}(2d-1,p)$ afterwards) by using the same edge variables, and
stop both at generation $n$.  The two differ only on the event $E_1$ that
some examined open edge leads to a vertex already in $S$, or on the
event $E_2$ that some vertex of generation $k\le n$ lies at graph
distance less than $k$ from the origin; on $E_1^c\cap E_2^c$ the first $n$
generations of the cluster form the branching tree with every vertex of
generation $k$ on $\partial B_k$, and then $\{0\leftrightarrow\partial B_n\}$
is exactly survival to generation $n$, since any open path to $\partial B_n$
has a vertex of generation at least $n$ whose ancestor at generation $n$
lies on $\partial B_n$.  A vertex $w$ examined from $v$ lies in $S$ only if
$w$ is a common neighbour of $v$ and of another vertex $u\in S$, and two
distinct vertices of $\Z^d$ have at most two common neighbours, so
$\Pp(E_1)\le2p\,\E|S|^2$; a vertex of generation $k$ is at distance less
than $k$ only if its path from the origin contains two opposite steps,
which for a given path of length $k$ has probability at most
$k^2/(2d)$ under the uniform choice of the $2d-1$ directions, so
$\Pp(E_2)\le\E[N]\,n^2/(2d)$ with $N$ the number of vertices of the tree
up to generation $n$.  Here $|S|\le N$, and $N$ is the total progeny to
generation $n$ of a branching process with offspring $\mathrm{Bin}(2d,p_c)$,
whose mean $2dp_c$ is bounded in $d$ by Kesten's
asymptotics; its second moment is bounded uniformly in
$d$ for fixed $n$.  Hence the two probabilities differ by $O_n(1/d)$, and
the survival probability of the branching process differs from $s_n$ by
$O_n(1/d)$ because $2dp_c=1+O(1/d)$ and the
binomial laws are within $O(1/d)$ of Poisson in total variation.
\end{proof}

\begin{conjecture}[Polynomial decay of the critical one-arm probability in every dimension]\label{av1}
For every $d\ge2$ the exponent $\rho_d$ exists: $\pi_d(n)=n^{-\rho_d+o(1)}$,
and $\pi_d$ is regularly varying, $\pi_d(2n)/\pi_d(n)\to2^{-\rho_d}$.
Moreover $\pi_d(n)\ge c_dn^{-2}$ for all $n$ and $d$, so that
$\rho_d\le2$, with $\rho_d<2$ for $2\le d\le5$ and $\rho_d=2$ for
$d\ge6$; and $\Pp_{p_c}(|C|\ge n)=n^{-1/\delta_d+o(1)}$ with
$\delta_d>2$ for $2\le d\le5$ and $\delta_d=2$ for $d\ge6$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Dimensional monotonicity of critical connectivity]\label{av2}
{\rm(i)} For every $n\ge1$ the critical one-arm probabilities decrease
strictly in the dimension: $\pi_{d+1}(n)<\pi_d(n)$ for all $d\ge2$, where
each $\pi_d$ is taken at its own critical point $p_c(\Z^d)$.
{\rm(ii)} The exponents are strictly increasing in the dimension below
the upper critical dimension: $\rho_2<\rho_3<\rho_4<\rho_5<\rho_6=2$, and
likewise $\beta_2<\dots<\beta_6=1$ and $\nu_2>\nu_3>\dots>\nu_6=1/2$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Finite clusters are largest at criticality]\label{av3}
{\rm(i)} For every $d\ge2$, $\sup_{p\in[0,1]}(\theta_n(p)-\theta(p))\asymp\pi_d(n)$,
and the supremum is attained at a point $p_n^*$ with
$n^{1/\nu_d}(p_n^*-p_c)$ bounded.
{\rm(ii)} For $2\le d\le5$ the supremum is $\pi_d(n)$ itself and is
attained only at $p_c$ (the case $d=6$ is left open): for every $n\ge1$ and every $p\ne p_c$,
\[
 \Pp_p\bigl(0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n,\ |C|<\infty\bigr)<\Pp_{p_c}\bigl(0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n\bigr).
\]
{\rm(iii)} For $d\ge7$ the ratio $\sup_p(\theta_n(p)-\theta(p))/\pi_d(n)$
converges to a constant $c_d\ge1$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Finite-size critical points and the pivotal exponent]\label{av4}
For every $d\ge2$ the correlation-length exponent $\nu_d$ exists, and:
{\rm(i)} the scaling function $\Phi_d$ exists, is continuous and
strictly increasing, and for every $\eps\in(0,1)$,
$n^{1/\nu_d}\bigl(p_n(\eps\,\pi_d(n))-p_c\bigr)\to\Phi_d^{-1}(\eps)$,
where $p_n$ is the finite-size threshold of Proposition~\ref{av:basic}(ii);
{\rm(ii)} the number of pivotal edges for the one-arm event satisfies
$\E_{p_c}\bigl[\#\mathrm{Piv}(0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n)\bigr]
=\theta_n'(p_c)=n^{1/\nu_d-\rho_d+o(1)}$;
{\rm(iii)} the width of the window in which $\theta_n$ rises from
$\eps\pi_d(n)$ to $\eps^{-1}\pi_d(n)$ is $n^{-1/\nu_d+o(1)}$, with the
same $\nu_d$ as the correlation length below $p_c$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Slab critical points]\label{av5}
For $d\ge3$ and $k\ge0$ let $S_k=\Z^{d-1}\times\{0,\dots,k\}$ and
$p_c(S_k)$ its critical point.  Then
$p_c(S_k)-p_c(\Z^d)\asymp k^{-1/\nu_d}$; more precisely
$k^{1/\nu_d}\bigl(p_c(S_k)-p_c(\Z^d)\bigr)\to a_d\in(0,\infty)$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Lipschitz dichotomy for the percolation probability at criticality]\label{av6}
As $p\downarrow p_c$, $\theta(p)/(p-p_c)\to\infty$ for $2\le d\le6$, while
for $d\ge7$ the ratio converges to a finite positive limit.
Equivalently, the mean-field bound $\theta(p)\ge c(p-p_c)$ is strict in
exponent for $d\le5$, strict by a divergent factor at $d=6$, and sharp
up to a constant for $d\ge7$: $\theta$ is Lipschitz at $p_c$ exactly
when $d\ge7$.  Equivalently, the triangle diagram
$\nabla(p_c)=\sum_{x,y}\tau_{p_c}(0,x)\tau_{p_c}(x,y)\tau_{p_c}(y,0)$ is
finite exactly when $d\ge7$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[No divergence of the specific heat]\label{av7}
For every $d\ge2$ the number of clusters per site $\kappa$ is twice
continuously differentiable on $[0,1]$, $\kappa''$ is H\"older
continuous at $p_c$, and $\kappa$ is not three times differentiable at
$p_c$: $\kappa'''$ diverges at $p_c$ like $|p-p_c|^{-1-\alpha_d}$ with
$\alpha_d=2-d\nu_d\in(-1,0)$ for $2\le d\le5$, and has a jump
discontinuity at $p_c$ for $d\ge7$; the case $d=6$ is left open.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Hyperscaling as a two-arm factorisation]\label{av8}
For $2\le d\le5$ there are constants $0<c_d\le C_d<\infty$ with
\[
 c_d\,\pi_d(|x|_\infty)^2\le\tau_{p_c}(0,x)\le C_d\,\pi_d(|x|_\infty)^2
 \qquad(x\ne0),
\]
so that $d-2+\eta_d=2\rho_d$; for $d=6$ the ratio
$\tau_{p_c}(0,x)/\pi_6(|x|_\infty)^2$ tends to zero like $1/\log|x|$; and
for $d\ge7$, $\tau_{p_c}(0,x)/\pi_d(|x|_\infty)^2\asymp|x|^{6-d}$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Sign of the anomalous dimension]\label{av9}
The exponent $\eta_d$ exists for every $d\ge2$, and
$|x|^{d-2}\tau_{p_c}(0,x)$ tends to zero for $d=2$, to infinity for
$3\le d\le6$, and to a finite positive limit for $d\ge7$: the anomalous
dimension is positive in the plane, negative in dimensions three to
five, and zero from six on, with a logarithmic divergence at $d=6$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Exceptional times of dynamical critical percolation]\label{av10}
Dynamical percolation on $\Z^d$ at $p_c$ has exceptional times for
$2\le d\le5$ and has almost surely none for $d\ge7$.  For $2\le d\le5$
the set of exceptional times has Hausdorff dimension $1-\beta_d$ almost
surely.  The case $d=6$ is left open.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Temporal correlations of the one-arm event]\label{av11}
Let $A_n=\{0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n\}$ and let $\omega_t$ be
dynamical percolation at $p_c$ on $\Z^d$.  For every $d\ge2$,
\[
 \Pp(\omega_0\in A_n,\ \omega_t\in A_n)\asymp
 \begin{cases}\pi_d(n),& t\le n^{-1/\nu_d},\\[2pt]
 \pi_d(n)^2\,t^{-\beta_d},& n^{-1/\nu_d}\le t\le1,\end{cases}
\]
uniformly in $n$ and $t$.  In the resampling formulation, if each edge
is resampled independently with probability $\eps$, the joint probability
of $A_n$ before and after the resampling is $\asymp\pi_d(n)$ when
$\eps\le n^{-1/\nu_d}$ and $\asymp\pi_d(n)^2\eps^{-\beta_d}$ when
$n^{-1/\nu_d}\le\eps\le1$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Logarithmic corrections in six dimensions]\label{av12}
On $\Z^6$, as $p\to p_c$,
\[
 \theta(p)=(p-p_c)\,|\log(p-p_c)|^{2/7+o(1)},\quad
 \chi(p)=(p_c-p)^{-1}|\log(p_c-p)|^{2/7+o(1)},\quad
 \xi(p)=(p_c-p)^{-1/2}|\log(p_c-p)|^{5/42+o(1)},
\]
and $\pi_6(n)=n^{-2}(\log n)^{11/21+o(1)}$, the exponent $11/21=5/21+2/7$
being what the one-parameter scaling relation $\pi_6(n)\approx\theta(p)$
at $\xi(p)=n$ gives, and the least secure of the four since logarithmic
corrections to hyperscaling at the critical dimension may modify it; in particular
$\theta(p)/(p-p_c)\to\infty$ and $|x|^4\tau_{p_c}(0,x)\to\infty$ on $\Z^6$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Site percolation dies at criticality]\label{av13}
For nearest-neighbour site percolation on $\Z^d$, $\theta^{\rm site}(p_c^{\rm site})=0$
for every $d\ge3$; moreover the site and bond models have the same
exponents, $\rho_d^{\rm site}=\rho_d$ and $\beta_d^{\rm site}=\beta_d$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Dying percolation on graphs of polynomial growth]\label{av14}
$\theta(p_c)=0$ for every connected quasi-transitive graph of
polynomial growth; in particular for the face-centred and body-centred
cubic lattices, for every Bravais lattice in $d\ge3$, and for the Cayley
graphs of finitely generated virtually nilpotent groups, the discrete
Heisenberg group first among them.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[The incipient infinite cluster in every dimension]\label{av15}
For every $d\ge2$: {\rm(i)} the limits
$\Pp_{p_c}(\,\cdot\mid0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n)$ as $n\to\infty$ and
$\Pp_p(\,\cdot\mid0\leftrightarrow\infty)$ as $p\downarrow p_c$ exist on
cylinder events and coincide, defining the incipient infinite cluster
measure $\Pp_{\rm IIC}$; {\rm(ii)} under $\Pp_{\rm IIC}$ the cluster of
the origin is almost surely infinite, is the unique infinite cluster,
and satisfies $|C\cap\Lambda_n|=n^{d_f+o(1)}$ with $d_f=d-\rho_d$ for
$2\le d\le6$ and $d_f=4$ for $d\ge6$; {\rm(iii)} simple
random walk on it has spectral dimension $d_s(d)\le4/3$, with equality
for $d\ge6$ and strict inequality at least for $d\in\{2,3\}$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Largest critical clusters in boxes and tori]\label{av16}
Let $C_{\max}(\Lambda_n)$ and $C_{\max}(\mathbb T_n^d)$ be the largest open
clusters at $p_c(\Z^d)$ in the box $\Lambda_n$ and in the torus
$(\Z/n\Z)^d$.  For $2\le d\le5$,
$|C_{\max}(\Lambda_n)|=n^{d-\rho_d+o(1)}$ and
$|C_{\max}(\mathbb T_n^d)|=n^{d-\rho_d+o(1)}$, with
$|C_{\max}(\mathbb T_n^d)|/|C_{\max}(\Lambda_n)|$ tight and bounded away
from zero; the case $d=6$, where the two exponents coincide but
logarithmic factors may separate the two sizes, is left open; for
$d\ge7$, $|C_{\max}(\Lambda_n)|=n^{4+o(1)}$ while
$|C_{\max}(\mathbb T_n^d)|=n^{2d/3+o(1)}$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Quasi-multiplicativity and box crossings in three dimensions]\label{av17}
On $\Z^3$ at $p_c$: {\rm(i)} there is $c>0$ with
$\Pp_{p_c}(0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_{2n}\mid0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n)\ge c$
for all $n$, so that $\pi_3(2n)\ge c\,\pi_3(n)$; {\rm(ii)} for every
$\lambda\ge1$ there is $c_\lambda>0$ such that the probability that the box
$[0,\lambda n]\times[0,n]^2$ is crossed in its long direction lies in
$[c_\lambda,1-c_\lambda]$ for all $n$; {\rm(iii)} for a smooth bounded
domain $D\subset\R^3$ with two disjoint boundary patches $A$ and $B$, the
probability that $A\cap nD$ is connected to $B\cap nD$ inside $nD$
converges as $n\to\infty$ to a limit in $(0,1)$ that is invariant under
Euclidean rotations and dilations of $(D,A,B)$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[The upper critical dimension is exactly six]\label{av18}
For nearest-neighbour bond percolation on $\Z^d$ the triangle diagram
$\nabla(p_c)=\sum_{x,y}\tau_{p_c}(0,x)\tau_{p_c}(x,y)\tau_{p_c}(y,0)$ is
finite if and only if $d\ge7$.  Consequently, for $7\le d\le10$ the
mean-field asymptotics hold: $\pi_d(n)\asymp n^{-2}$,
$\theta(p)\asymp p-p_c$, $\chi(p)\asymp(p_c-p)^{-1}$,
$\tau_{p_c}(0,x)\asymp|x|^{-(d-2)}$, and the incipient infinite cluster
exists with the properties of; while for
$d\le6$, $\nabla(p_c)=\infty$.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Half-space one-arm exponents]\label{av19}
For every $d\ge2$ let $H=\Z^{d-1}\times\Z_{\ge0}$, whose critical point
equals $p_c(\Z^d)$, and let
$\pi_d^+(n)=\Pp_{p_c}(0\leftrightarrow\partial\Lambda_n\cap H\text{ by an open path in }H)$
be the half-space one-arm probability from the origin on the boundary
hyperplane.  Then $\pi_d^+(n)$ has an exponent $\rho_d^+$, with $\rho_d^+>\rho_d$, $\rho_d^+$
nondecreasing in $d$, $\rho_2^+=1/3$ for the triangular lattice, and
$\rho_d^+=3$ for $d\ge7$; the surface excess $\rho_d^+-\rho_d$ is
nondecreasing in $d$, from $11/48$ in the plane to $1$ in high
dimensions.
\end{conjecture}


\begin{conjecture}[Critical crossing probability of the box]\label{av20}
Let $R_d(n)=\Pp_{p_c}(\Lambda_n\text{ is crossed from left to right})$, the
box carrying free boundary conditions.
Then $R_d(n)$ converges as $n\to\infty$ to a limit $R_d$, with
$R_2=1/2$, $R_d\in(0,1)$ for $3\le d\le5$, and $R_d=1$ for $d\ge6$;
moreover the number of distinct crossing clusters is tight for
$2\le d\le5$, grows like $\log n$ for $d=6$, and like $n^{d-6+o(1)}$ for
$d\ge7$.
\end{conjecture}
